# Harness Prompt

Guidelines applied to every session:

- Only create or modify the specific file(s) the user asked about. Do NOT rewrite or improve other files unless explicitly asked.
- After completing the requested change, STOP and summarize what you did in a very short summary. Do not be verbose. Do not re-read or re-write files to verify.
- Each file should be written at most once per request unless the user asks for a revision.
- A successful write_file returns "Written: <path>". Trust it; do not read the file back to confirm.

Available skills (call use_skill with the exact name to load full instructions):
- create-skill: Create a reusable skill (SKILL.md) that packages a workflow.
- design: When doing web design use this skill
- engineering: When writing code in a programming language, PHP, Go, Rust, Python, JaveScript/TypeScript, etc...
- math-formulas: Write mathematical/scientific formulas in chat, markdown files, kanban cards, agent streams, or mindmap node labels — Coder renders LaTeX math via KaTeX. Use when a response needs an equation, formula, or scientific notation instead of ASCII approximations.
- mermaid-mindmap: Create or edit a `.mindmap.md` mindmap file that Coder renders as an interactive canvas (drag nodes, pan/zoom, export to PNG/PDF).
```

Art by our little drawing bot.</figcaption>\n    </figure>\n\n    <article>\n      <p>\n        There's a myth floating around the web: that to be a \"serious\" engineer you must\n        pick <em>one</em> language and marry it for life. Pick React and shun the backend.\n        Pick Go and scoff at anything interpreted. Pick PHP and… well, the internet has\n        opinions. But here's the truth. React, PHP, and Go aren't rivals fighting for a\n        throne. They're <strong>friends</strong> — each great at what they do, and they slot\n        together beautifully on a modern stack.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>The Friendship, Explained</h2>\n      <p>\n        In the image above, you'll notice each mascot brings its own little piece to one\n        shared puzzle: the atom-core energy of React, the gentle elephant wisdom of PHP, and\n        the spunky little gopher from Go. They're holding hands, sliding gears into place.\n        That's not a coincidence — it's the exact shape of a well-architected product.\n      </p>\n\n      <div class=\"personas\">\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot react\"></span>React</h4>\n          <p>The friendly face. Handles the UI, keeps the interface lively and responsive.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot php\"></span>PHP</h4>\n          <p>The workhorse. Your template-first pages, server-side rendering, and glue.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot go\"></span>Go</h4>\n          <p>The powerhouse. Fast APIs, concurrency, and the heavy lifting under the hood.</p>\n        </div>\n      </div>\n\n      <h2>Why They Play Nicely Together</h2>\n      <p>\n        The secret to great teams — human or code — is a clear division of labor. Let's see\n        where each language shines and how the others fill the gaps.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>React owns the interface</h3>\n      <p>\n        React is king of declarative UI. Components, state, and that buttery-smooth\n        re-rendering are perfect for anything interactive — dashboards, editors, apps. When\n        a user needs to <em>feel</em> an interface, React is your friend.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>PHP owns the content</h3>\n      <p>\n        PHP still powers a huge portion of the web, and it's unfairly good at what it does:\n        turning HTML on the server. It's the backbone for content sites, blogs, and CMSes.\n        You don't need a 200-megabyte toolchain to ship a page — PHP just works.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>Go owns the scale</h3>\n      <p>\n        When you need to move a million requests without breaking a sweat, Go's lightweight\n        goroutines and compiled speed come through. It's the perfect choice for APIs,\n        background workers, and services that have to stay up.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>A Realistic Pattern</h2>\n      <p>\n        Here's how these friends might actually sit together in one application:\n      </p>\n\n      <ul>\n        <li><strong>Go</strong> exposes a fast JSON API for heavy operations.</li>\n        <li><strong>PHP</strong> serves the pages people discover and read.</li>\n        <li><strong>React</strong> builds the interactive bits on top of both.</li>\n      </ul>\n\n      <pre><code>// Go: a snappy API endpoint\nhttp.HandleFunc(\"/api/users\", func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {\n    json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(users)\n})</code></pre>\n\n      <blockquote>\n        Use the <strong>right tool for the job</strong>, not the tool you're most loyal to. Friends\n        don't compete — they delegate.\n      </blockquote>\n\n      <p>\n        Is it more complex than one monolith trained to do everything? Sometimes. Art by our little drawing bot.</figcaption>\n    </figure>\n\n    <article>\n      <p>\n        There's a myth floating around the web: that to be a \"serious\" engineer you must\n        pick <em>one</em> language and marry it for life. React, PHP, and Go aren't rivals fighting for a\n        throne. They're <strong>friends</strong> — each great at what they do, and they slot\n        together beautifully on a modern stack.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>The Friendship, Explained</h2>\n      <p>\n        In the image above, you'll notice each mascot brings its own little piece to one\n        shared puzzle: the atom-core energy of React, the gentle elephant wisdom of PHP, and\n        the spunky little gopher from Go. They're holding hands, sliding gears into place.\n        That's not a coincidence — it's the exact shape of a well-architected product.\n      </p>\n\n      <div class=\"personas\">\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot react\"></span>React</h4>\n          <p>The friendly face. Handles the UI, keeps the interface lively and responsive.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot php\"></span>PHP</h4>\n          <p>The workhorse. Your template-first pages, server-side rendering, and glue.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot go\"></span>Go</h4>\n          <p>The powerhouse. Fast APIs, concurrency, and the heavy lifting under the hood.</p>\n        </div>\n      </div>\n\n      <h2>Why They Play Nicely Together</h2>\n      <p>\n        The secret to great teams — human or code — is a clear division of labor. Let's see\n        where each language shines and how the others fill the gaps.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>React owns the interface</h3>\n      <p>\n        React is king of declarative UI. Components, state, and that buttery-smooth\n        re-rendering are perfect for anything interactive — dashboards, editors, apps. When\n        a user needs to <em>feel</em> an interface, React is your friend.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>PHP owns the content</h3>\n      <p>\n        PHP still powers a huge portion of the web, and it's unfairly good at what it does:\n        turning HTML on the server. It's the perfect choice for APIs,\n        background workers, and services that have to stay up.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>A Realistic Pattern</h2>\n      <p>\n        Here's how these friends might actually sit together in one application:\n      </p>\n\n      <ul>\n        <li><strong>Go</strong> exposes a fast JSON API for heavy operations.</li>\n        <li><strong>PHP</strong> serves the pages people discover and read.</li>\n        <li><strong>React</strong> builds the interactive bits on top of both.</li>\n      </ul>\n\n      <pre><code>// Go: a snappy API endpoint\nhttp.HandleFunc(\"/api/users\", func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {\n    json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(users)\n})</code></pre>\n\n      <blockquote>\n        Use the <strong>right tool for the job</strong>, not the tool you're most loyal to. Friends\n        don't compete — they delegate.\n      </blockquote>\n\n      <p>\n        Is it more complex than one monolith trained to do everything? Sometimes. But the\n        result is a system where each language does what it's genuinely best at, and the\n        whole thing moves faster than any of them could alone.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>Stop Choosing. They're <strong>friends</strong> — each great at what they do, and they slot\n        together beautifully on a modern stack.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>The Friendship, Explained</h2>\n      <p>\n        In the image above, you'll notice each mascot brings its own little piece to one\n        shared puzzle: the atom-core energy of React, the gentle elephant wisdom of PHP, and\n        the spunky little gopher from Go. They're holding hands, sliding gears into place.\n        That's not a coincidence — it's the exact shape of a well-architected product.\n      </p>\n\n      <div class=\"personas\">\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot react\"></span>React</h4>\n          <p>The friendly face. Handles the UI, keeps the interface lively and responsive.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot php\"></span>PHP</h4>\n          <p>The workhorse. Your template-first pages, server-side rendering, and glue.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot go\"></span>Go</h4>\n          <p>The powerhouse. Fast APIs, concurrency, and the heavy lifting under the hood.</p>\n        </div>\n      </div>\n\n      <h2>Why They Play Nicely Together</h2>\n      <p>\n        The secret to great teams — human or code — is a clear division of labor. Let's see\n        where each language shines and how the others fill the gaps.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>React owns the interface</h3>\n      <p>\n        React is king of declarative UI. Components, state, and that buttery-smooth\n        re-rendering are perfect for anything interactive — dashboards, editors, apps. When\n        a user needs to <em>feel</em> an interface, React is your friend.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>PHP owns the content</h3>\n      <p>\n        PHP still powers a huge portion of the web, and it's unfairly good at what it does:\n        turning HTML on the server. It's the backbone for content sites, blogs, and CMSes.\n        You don't need a 200-megabyte toolchain to ship a page — PHP just works.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>Go owns the scale</h3>\n      <p>\n        When you need to move a million requests without breaking a sweat, Go's lightweight\n        goroutines and compiled speed come through. It's the perfect choice for APIs,\n        background workers, and services that have to stay up.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>A Realistic Pattern</h2>\n      <p>\n        Here's how these friends might actually sit together in one application:\n      </p>\n\n      <ul>\n        <li><strong>Go</strong> exposes a fast JSON API for heavy operations.</li>\n        <li><strong>PHP</strong> serves the pages people discover and read.</li>\n        <li><strong>React</strong> builds the interactive bits on top of both.</li>\n      </ul>\n\n      <pre><code>// Go: a snappy API endpoint\nhttp.HandleFunc(\"/api/users\", func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {\n    json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(users)\n})</code></pre>\n\n      <blockquote>\n        Use the <strong>right tool for the job</strong>, not the tool you're most loyal to. Friends\n        don't compete — they delegate.\n      </blockquote>\n\n      <p>\n        Is it more complex than one monolith trained to do everything? Sometimes. Start Teaming.</h2>\n      <p>\n        The takeaway is simple: don't let fanboy wars drive your architecture. Be like the\n        three friends in the picture. - math-formulas: Write mathematical/scientific formulas in chat, markdown files, kanban cards, agent streams, or mindmap node labels — Coder renders LaTeX math via KaTeX. Use when a response needs an equation, formula, or scientific notation instead of ASCII approximations.
- mermaid-mindmap: Create or edit a `.mindmap.md` mindmap file that Coder renders as an interactive canvas (drag nodes, pan/zoom, export to PNG/PDF).
```

Art by our little drawing bot.</figcaption>\n    </figure>\n\n    <article>\n      <p>\n        There's a myth floating around the web: that to be a \"serious\" engineer you must\n        pick <em>one</em> language and marry it for life. React, PHP, and Go aren't rivals fighting for a\n        throne. They're <strong>friends</strong> — each great at what they do, and they slot\n        together beautifully on a modern stack.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>The Friendship, Explained</h2>\n      <p>\n        In the image above, you'll notice each mascot brings its own little piece to one\n        shared puzzle: the atom-core energy of React, the gentle elephant wisdom of PHP, and\n        the spunky little gopher from Go. They're holding hands, sliding gears into place.\n        That's not a coincidence — it's the exact shape of a well-architected product.\n      </p>\n\n      <div class=\"personas\">\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot react\"></span>React</h4>\n          <p>The friendly face. Handles the UI, keeps the interface lively and responsive.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot php\"></span>PHP</h4>\n          <p>The workhorse. Your template-first pages, server-side rendering, and glue.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot go\"></span>Go</h4>\n          <p>The powerhouse. Fast APIs, concurrency, and the heavy lifting under the hood.</p>\n        </div>\n      </div>\n\n      <h2>Why They Play Nicely Together</h2>\n      <p>\n        The secret to great teams — human or code — is a clear division of labor. Let's see\n        where each language shines and how the others fill the gaps.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>React owns the interface</h3>\n      <p>\n        React is king of declarative UI. Components, state, and that buttery-smooth\n        re-rendering are perfect for anything interactive — dashboards, editors, apps. When\n        a user needs to <em>feel</em> an interface, React is your friend.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>PHP owns the content</h3>\n      <p>\n        PHP still powers a huge portion of the web, and it's unfairly good at what it does:\n        turning HTML on the server. It's the backbone for content sites, blogs, and CMSes.\n        You don't need a 200-megabyte toolchain to ship a page — PHP just works.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>Go owns the scale</h3>\n      <p>\n        When you need to move a million requests without breaking a sweat, Go's lightweight\n        goroutines and compiled speed come through. It's the perfect choice for APIs,\n        background workers, and services that have to stay up.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>A Realistic Pattern</h2>\n      <p>\n        Here's how these friends might actually sit together in one application:\n      </p>\n\n      <ul>\n        <li><strong>Go</strong> exposes a fast JSON API for heavy operations.</li>\n        <li><strong>PHP</strong> serves the pages people discover and read.</li>\n        <li><strong>React</strong> builds the interactive bits on top of both.</li>\n      </ul>\n\n      <pre><code>// Go: a snappy API endpoint\nhttp.HandleFunc(\"/api/users\", func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {\n    json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(users)\n})</code></pre>\n\n      <blockquote>\n        Use the <strong>right tool for the job</strong>, not the tool you're most loyal to. Friends\n        don't compete — they delegate.\n      </blockquote>\n\n      <p>\n        Is it more complex than one monolith trained to do everything? Sometimes. But the\n        result is a system where each language does what it's genuinely best at, and the\n        whole thing moves faster than any of them could alone.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>Stop Choosing. margin: 0 auto; padding: 0 20px; }\n\n    .kicker {\n      display: inline-block;\n      font-size: 0.8rem;\n      letter-spacing: 0.12em;\n      text-transform: uppercase;\n      background: rgba(255, 255, 255, 0.18);\n      padding: 4px 12px;\n      border-radius: 999px;\n      margin-bottom: 18px;\n    }\n\n    h1 {\n      margin: 0 0 12px;\n      font-size: 2.1rem;\n      line-height: 1.25;\n      letter-spacing: -0.02em;\n    }\n\n    .meta {\n      font-size: 0.95rem;\n      opacity: 0.9;\n    }\n\n    .meta span { margin-right: 16px; }\n\n    h2 {\n      margin-top: 2.2em;\n      font-size: 1.5rem;\n      letter-spacing: -0.01em;\n      border-bottom: 2px solid var(--border);\n      padding-bottom: 8px;\n    }\n\n    h3 { margin-top: 1.6em; font-size: 1.12rem; }\n\n    p { margin: 16px 0; }\n\n    ul, ol { padding-left: 24px; }\n\n    li { margin: 8px 0; }\n\n    code {\n      background: #eef0f7;\n      border: 1px solid var(--border);\n      border-radius: 6px;\n      padding: 2px 6px;\n      font-family: \"SF Mono\", \"Menlo\", \"Consolas\", monospace;\n      font-size: 0.9em;\n    }\n\n    pre {\n      background: #1b1f2b;\n      color: #e6edf3;\n      padding: 18px;\n      border-radius: var(--radius);\n      overflow-x: auto;\n      font-size: 0.9rem;\n      line-height: 1.6;\n    }\n\n    pre code {\n      background: none;\n      border: none;\n      color: inherit;\n      padding: 0;\n    }\n\n    pre .lang {\n      display: block;\n      font-size: 0.75rem;\n      letter-spacing: 0.1em;\n      text-transform: uppercase;\n      color: #8b93a7;\n      margin-bottom: 10px;\n    }\n\n    blockquote {\n      margin: 24px 0;\n      padding: 14px 20px;\n      border-left: 4px solid var(--accent);\n      background: #e4f6fb;\n      border-radius: 0 var(--radius) var(--radius) 0;\n      color: var(--muted);\n    }\n\n    .note {\n      background: var(--surface);\n      border: 1px solid var(--border);\n      border-left: 4px solid var(--php);\n      border-radius: var(--radius);\n      padding: 14px 18px;\n      margin: 20px 0;\n      font-size: 0.93rem;\n      color: var(--muted);\n    }\n\n    table.timeline {\n      width: 100%;\n      border-collapse: collapse;\n      margin: 20px 0;\n      font-size: 0.92rem;\n    }\n\n    table.timeline th, table.timeline td {\n      text-align: left;\n      padding: 10px 12px;\n      border-bottom: 1px solid var(--border);\n    }\n\n    table.timeline th { color: var(--muted); font-size: 0.8rem; text-transform: uppercase; letter-spacing: 0.06em; }\n\n    footer {\n      border-top: 1px solid var(--border);\n      padding: 28px 0 40px;\n      color: var(--muted);\n      font-size: 0.9rem;\n      text-align: center;\n      background: var(--surface);\n      margin-top: 40px;\n    }\n  </style>\n</head>\n<body>\n  <header class=\"hero\">\n    <div class=\"hero-inner\">\n      <span class=\"kicker\">Go × PHP · Microservices</span>\n      <h1>Go for Long-Running Processes, Called From PHP</h1>\n      <div class=\"meta\">\n        <span>✍️ Jeffrey</span>\n        <span>📅 Aug 10, 2026</span>\n        <span>⏱ 8 min read</span>\n      </div>\n    </div>\n  </header>\n\n  <div class=\"container\">\n    <article>\n      <p>\n        PHP is an old friend. It starts, does its job, and exits — usually in milliseconds.\n        That lifecycle is what makes it so reliable for web requests. But it's also a\n        <strong>limitation</strong>. Some tasks aren't finished in a single request. Some\n        jobs need to keep running — polling a queue, processing files, waiting for a WebSocket\n        connection — long after PHP has packed up and gone home.\n      </p>\n\n      <p>\n        That's where Go steps in. We already established that Go makes a great friend. Now\n        let's give it a <em>job</em>: run a long-lived process, and let PHP knock on its door\n        whenever something needs doing.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>Why a Long-Running Process Can't Live in PHP</h2>\n      <p>\n        Every PHP request is effectively born and dies in the time it takes to render one\n        page. There's no persistent memory between requests by default, and holding a request\n        open for minutes just to keep a worker alive is a recipe for timeouts, exhausted\n        connections, and grumpy users.\n      </p>\n\n      <p>Meanwhile, Go is built for exactly this:</p>\n\n      <ul>\n        <li><strong>It keeps running.</strong> A Go server boots once and stays up for months.</li>\n        <li><strong>It handles concurrency.</strong> Goroutines juggle thousands of tasks cheaply.</li>\n        <li><strong>It's fast.</strong> Compiled and near-instant startup, no interpreter overhead.</li>\n        <li><strong>It's memory-aware.</strong> You can hold state, caches, and connections in one place.</li>\n      </ul>\n\n      <h2>The Architecture: A Go Microservice</h2>\n      <p>\n        Instead of asking PHP to babysit a long job, we run a standalone Go service. PHP calls\n        it over HTTP. The Go service absorbs the long-running work, and PHP gets on with\n        answering requests. They never have to care about each other's internal lives.\n      </p>\n\n      <table class=\"timeline\">\n        <thead>\n          <tr>\n            <th>Step</th>\n            <th>Who</th>\n            <th>What happens</th>\n          </tr>\n        </thead>\n        <tbody>\n          <tr>\n            <td>1</td>\n            <td>PHP</td>\n            <td>A user submits a heavy job to your web app.</td>\n          </tr>\n          <tr>\n            <td>2</td>\n            <td>PHP → Go</td>\n            <td>PHP sends the job to your Go microservice via HTTP.</td>\n          </tr>\n          <tr>\n            <td>3</td>\n            <td>Go</td>\n            <td>Go starts the long task and returns a job ID immediately.</td>\n          </tr>\n          <tr>\n            <td>4</td>\n            <td>PHP</td>\n            <td>PHP replies to the user right away — no blocking wait.</td>\n          </tr>\n          <tr>\n            <td>5</td>\n            <td>Go</td>\n            <td>Go keeps processing while the web keeps breathing.</td>\n          </tr>\n        </tbody>\n      </table>\n\n      <h2>A Minimal Go Service</h2>\n      <p>\n        Here's a tiny example. The Go service accepts a job, kicks it off in the background,\n        and answers immediately. No waiting around.\n      </p>\n\n      <pre><code><span class=\"lang\">Go — microservice.go</span>package main\n\nimport (\n    \"encoding/json\"\n    \"fmt\"\n    \"log\"\n    \"net/http\"\n    \"time\"\n)\n\ntype Job struct {\n    ID   string `json:\"id\"`\n    Data string `json:\"data\"`\n}\n\nvar results = make(map[string]string)\n\n// simulate a long-running task in a goroutine\nfunc process(job Job) {\n    time.Sleep(10 * time.Second) // pretend this is real work\n    results[job.ID] = \"done with: \" + job.Data\n}\n\nfunc submitHandler(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {\n    var job Job\n    if err := json.NewDecoder(r.Body).Decode(&job); err != nil {\n        http.Error(w, err.Error(), http.StatusBadRequest)\n        return\n    }\n    job.ID = fmt.Sprintf(\"job-%d\", time.Now().UnixNano())\n    go process(job) // returns immediately\n\n    w.Header().Set(\"Content-Type\", \"application/json\")\n    json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(map[string]string{\"job_id\": job.ID})\n}\n\nfunc statusHandler(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {\n    id := r.URL.Query().Get(\"id\")\n    result, ok := results[id]\n    if !ok {\n        http.Error(w, \"not done yet\", http.StatusAccepted)\n        return\n    }\n    json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(map[string]string{\"status\": result})\n}\n\nfunc main() {\n    http.HandleFunc(\"/submit\", submitHandler)\n    http.HandleFunc(\"/status\", statusHandler)\n    log.Println(\"Go microservice listening on :8080\")\n    log.Fatal(http.ListenAndServe(\":8080\", nil))\n}</code></pre>\n\n      <p>\n        Notice the <code>go process(job)</code> line. That single keyword spins the task off\n        into a goroutine so the service can serve the next request immediately. Ten seconds of\n        work happens <em>while</em> PHP keeps talking to users.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>Calling It From PHP</h2>\n      <p>\n        Now the fun part. PHP just makes HTTP calls using cURL — something it's done happily\n        for a decade. Two calls: one to <em>submit</em> the work, and one to <em>check the\n        status</em>.\n      </p>\n\n      <pre><code><span class=\"lang\">PHP — submit.php</span>&lt;?php\n\nfunction go_submit(string $data): string {\n    $ch = curl_init('http://localhost:8080/submit');\n\n    curl_setopt($ch, CURLOPT_RETURNTRANSFER, true);\n    curl_setopt($ch, CURLOPT_POST, true);\n    curl_setopt($ch, CURLOPT_HTTPHEADER, ['Content-Type: application/json']);\n    curl_setopt($ch, CURLOPT_POSTFIELDS, json_encode(['data' => $data]));\n\n    $out = curl_exec($ch);\n    curl_close($ch);\n\n    $decoded = json_decode($out, true);\n    return $decoded['job_id'] ?? '';\n}\n\nfunction go_status(string $jobId): ?string {\n    $ch = curl_init('http://localhost:8080/status?id=' . urlencode($jobId));\n\n    curl_setopt($ch, CURLOPT_RETURNTRANSFER, true);\n    $out = curl_exec($ch);\n    curl_close($ch);\n\n    return json_decode($out, true)['status'] ?? null;\n}</code></pre>\n\n      <p>\n        And that's it. `go_submit()` fires the job and gets back an ID almost instantly.\n        `go_status()` polls for the result. Both PHP functions stay fast because the heavy\n        lifting lives in the Go process, not in the web thread.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>Wiring It Into a Page</h2>\n      <p>\n        A typical flow in your PHP app looks like this:\n      </p>\n\n      <pre><code><span class=\"lang\">PHP — handle_request.php</span>&lt;?php\n\n$upload = $_POST['file_to_process'] ?? '';\n\n// Hand off the long job to Go, get an ID fast.\n$jobId = go_submit($upload);\n\n// Show a \"processing…\" screen while Go does the work.\necho \"Your job is queued! ID: {$jobId}\";\n\n// Later, poll for the result:\n// $status = go_status($jobId);</code></pre>\n\n      <blockquote>\n        The user sees an instant response. The long task runs safely in its own process.\n        PHP stays snappy. Go stays busy. Everyone wins.\n      </blockquote>\n\n      <h2>Operational Notes for the Real World</h2>\n      <p>\n        A toy example is nice, but a friendly microservice in production deserves a little\n        more care:\n      </p>\n\n      <ul>\n        <li><strong>Run it separately.</strong> Use a systemd service, Docker container, or\n            supervisor so Go restarts if it crashes.</li>\n        <li><strong>Persist state.</strong> If you need results to survive a restart, back them\n            up to a database instead of an in-memory map.</li>\n        <li><strong>Secure it.</strong> Bind Go to <code>localhost</code> or a private network\n            so only your PHP app can reach it — or protect the endpoint with a shared token.</li>\n        <li><strong>Add timeouts.</strong> Set reasonable cURL timeouts in PHP and cancel\n            long goroutines that are no longer needed.</li>\n        <li><strong>Scale horizontally.</strong> Because PHP and Go communicate over plain\n            HTTP, you can run many Go workers behind a load balancer when demand grows.</li>\n      </ul>\n\n      <div class=\"note\">\n        <strong>Prefer something even lighter?</strong> If your job is truly background-only,\n        PHP can enqueue it into a message queue (like Redis or RabbitMQ) and a Go worker can\n        consume from the other side. The HTTP microservice is the simplest jumping-off point,\n        but the same friendship scales down — or up — to whatever fits.\n      </div>\n\n      <h2>Friends in Different Time Zones</h2>\n      <p>\n        PHP and Go don't live in the same process, and that's exactly the point. Each runs on\n        the schedule and in the environment it's built for. Go stays awake for the long haul;\n        PHP answers each visitor cheerfully and moves on. Split responsibilities, no jealousy,\n        no arguments.\n      </p>\n\n      <p>\n        That, right there, is what it means for two languages to be friends: let the one that\n        thrives on endurance handle the long-running work, and let the one that thrives on\n        quick hellos handle the front door.\n      </p>\n    </article>\n  </div>\n\n  <footer>\n    <div class=\"container\">\n      © 2026 language-tests · Go keeps running, PHP keeps charming. \n    </div>\n  </footer>\n</body>\n</html>\n"}
```

Tech</span>\n      <h1>React, PHP &amp; Go: Three Best Friends Who Actually Work Great Together</h1>\n      <div class=\"meta\">\n        <span>✍️ Jeffrey</span>\n        <span>📅 Aug 10, 2026</span>\n        <span>⏱ 6 min read</span>\n      </div>\n    </div>\n  </header>\n\n  <div class=\"container\">\n    <figure>\n      <img src=\"../images/react-php-go-friends.png\" alt=\"React, PHP, and Go mascots as friendly teammates working together\">\n      <figcaption>Three different tools, one happy team. Art by our little drawing bot.</figcaption>\n    </figure>\n\n    <article>\n      <p>\n        There's a myth floating around the web: that to be a \"serious\" engineer you must\n        pick <em>one</em> language and marry it for life. Pick React and shun the backend.\n        Pick Go and scoff at anything interpreted. Pick PHP and… well, the internet has\n        opinions. But here's the truth. React, PHP, and Go aren't rivals fighting for a\n        throne. They're <strong>friends</strong> — each great at what they do, and they slot\n        together beautifully on a modern stack.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>The Friendship, Explained</h2>\n      <p>\n        In the image above, you'll notice each mascot brings its own little piece to one\n        shared puzzle: the atom-core energy of React, the gentle elephant wisdom of PHP, and\n        the spunky little gopher from Go. They're holding hands, sliding gears into place.\n        That's not a coincidence — it's the exact shape of a well-architected product.\n      </p>\n\n      <div class=\"personas\">\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot react\"></span>React</h4>\n          <p>The friendly face. Handles the UI, keeps the interface lively and responsive.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot php\"></span>PHP</h4>\n          <p>The workhorse. Your template-first pages, server-side rendering, and glue.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot go\"></span>Go</h4>\n          <p>The powerhouse. Fast APIs, concurrency, and the heavy lifting under the hood.</p>\n        </div>\n      </div>\n\n      <h2>Why They Play Nicely Together</h2>\n      <p>\n        The secret to great teams — human or code — is a clear division of labor. Let's see\n        where each language shines and how the others fill the gaps.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>React owns the interface</h3>\n      <p>\n        React is king of declarative UI. Components, state, and that buttery-smooth\n        re-rendering are perfect for anything interactive — dashboards, editors, apps. When\n        a user needs to <em>feel</em> an interface, React is your friend.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>PHP owns the content</h3>\n      <p>\n        PHP still powers a huge portion of the web, and it's unfairly good at what it does:\n        turning HTML on the server. It's the backbone for content sites, blogs, and CMSes.\n        You don't need a 200-megabyte toolchain to ship a page — PHP just works.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>Go owns the scale</h3>\n      <p>\n        When you need to move a million requests without breaking a sweat, Go's lightweight\n        goroutines and compiled speed come through. It's the perfect choice for APIs,\n        background workers, and services that have to stay up.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>A Realistic Pattern</h2>\n      <p>\n        Here's how these friends might actually sit together in one application:\n      </p>\n\n      <ul>\n        <li><strong>Go</strong> exposes a fast JSON API for heavy operations.</li>\n        <li><strong>PHP</strong> serves the pages people discover and read.</li>\n        <li><strong>React</strong> builds the interactive bits on top of both.</li>\n      </ul>\n\n      <pre><code>// Go: a snappy API endpoint\nhttp.HandleFunc(\"/api/users\", func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {\n    json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(users)\n})</code></pre>\n\n      <blockquote>\n        Use the <strong>right tool for the job</strong>, not the tool you're most loyal to. Friends\n        don't compete — they delegate.\n      </blockquote>\n\n      <p>\n        Is it more complex than one monolith trained to do everything? Sometimes. But the\n        result is a system where each language does what it's genuinely best at, and the\n        whole thing moves faster than any of them could alone.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>Stop Choosing. Start Teaming.</h2>\n      <p>\n        The takeaway is simple: don't let fanboy wars drive your architecture. Be like the\n        three friends in the picture. Keep your stack open-minded, let each language shine\n        in its lane, and you'll find that React, PHP, and Go aren't just compatible —\n        they're a delight.\n      </p>\n\n      <p>\n        Now go build something cool with your whole squad. 🚀\n      </p>\n    </article>\n  </div>\n\n  <footer>\n    <div class=\"container\">\n      © 2026 language-tests · Written because tech doesn't have to fight.\n    </div>\n  </footer>\n</body>\n</html>\n"}}]},{"role":"tool","content":[{"type":"tool-result","toolCallId":"call_5aa5e7edcec244deaef54a68","toolName":"write_file","output":{"type":"text","value":"Written: /Users/jeffrey/www/language-tests/articles/react-php-go-friends.html"}}]},{"role":"assistant","content":"Created `articles/react-php-go-friends.html` — a complete styled blog post referencing the image (via `../images/react-php-go-friends.png`). Tech</span>\n      <h1>React, PHP &amp; Go: Three Best Friends Who Actually Work Great Together</h1>\n      <div class=\"meta\">\n        <span>✍️ Jeffrey</span>\n        <span>📅 Aug 10, 2026</span>\n        <span>⏱ 6 min read</span>\n      </div>\n    </div>\n  </header>\n\n  <div class=\"container\">\n    <figure>\n      <img src=\"../images/react-php-go-friends.png\" alt=\"React, PHP, and Go mascots as friendly teammates working together\">\n      <figcaption>Three different tools, one happy team. Art by our little drawing bot.</figcaption>\n    </figure>\n\n    <article>\n      <p>\n        There's a myth floating around the web: that to be a \"serious\" engineer you must\n        pick <em>one</em> language and marry it for life. Pick React and shun the backend.\n        Pick Go and scoff at anything interpreted. Pick PHP and… well, the internet has\n        opinions. But here's the truth. React, PHP, and Go aren't rivals fighting for a\n        throne. They're <strong>friends</strong> — each great at what they do, and they slot\n        together beautifully on a modern stack.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>The Friendship, Explained</h2>\n      <p>\n        In the image above, you'll notice each mascot brings its own little piece to one\n        shared puzzle: the atom-core energy of React, the gentle elephant wisdom of PHP, and\n        the spunky little gopher from Go. They're holding hands, sliding gears into place.\n        That's not a coincidence — it's the exact shape of a well-architected product.\n      </p>\n\n      <div class=\"personas\">\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot react\"></span>React</h4>\n          <p>The friendly face. Handles the UI, keeps the interface lively and responsive.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot php\"></span>PHP</h4>\n          <p>The workhorse. Your template-first pages, server-side rendering, and glue.</p>\n        </div>\n        <div class=\"persona\">\n          <h4><span class=\"dot go\"></span>Go</h4>\n          <p>The powerhouse. Fast APIs, concurrency, and the heavy lifting under the hood.</p>\n        </div>\n      </div>\n\n      <h2>Why They Play Nicely Together</h2>\n      <p>\n        The secret to great teams — human or code — is a clear division of labor. Let's see\n        where each language shines and how the others fill the gaps.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>React owns the interface</h3>\n      <p>\n        React is king of declarative UI. Components, state, and that buttery-smooth\n        re-rendering are perfect for anything interactive — dashboards, editors, apps. When\n        a user needs to <em>feel</em> an interface, React is your friend.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>PHP owns the content</h3>\n      <p>\n        PHP still powers a huge portion of the web, and it's unfairly good at what it does:\n        turning HTML on the server. It's the backbone for content sites, blogs, and CMSes.\n        You don't need a 200-megabyte toolchain to ship a page — PHP just works.\n      </p>\n\n      <h3>Go owns the scale</h3>\n      <p>\n        When you need to move a million requests without breaking a sweat, Go's lightweight\n        goroutines and compiled speed come through. It's the perfect choice for APIs,\n        background workers, and services that have to stay up.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>A Realistic Pattern</h2>\n      <p>\n        Here's how these friends might actually sit together in one application:\n      </p>\n\n      <ul>\n        <li><strong>Go</strong> exposes a fast JSON API for heavy operations.</li>\n        <li><strong>PHP</strong> serves the pages people discover and read.</li>\n        <li><strong>React</strong> builds the interactive bits on top of both.</li>\n      </ul>\n\n      <pre><code>// Go: a snappy API endpoint\nhttp.HandleFunc(\"/api/users\", func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {\n    json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(users)\n})</code></pre>\n\n      <blockquote>\n        Use the <strong>right tool for the job</strong>, not the tool you're most loyal to. Friends\n        don't compete — they delegate.\n      </blockquote>\n\n      <p>\n        Is it more complex than one monolith trained to do everything? Sometimes. But the\n        result is a system where each language does what it's genuinely best at, and the\n        whole thing moves faster than any of them could alone.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>Stop Choosing. Start Teaming.</h2>\n      <p>\n        The takeaway is simple: don't let fanboy wars drive your architecture. Be like the\n        three friends in the picture. Keep your stack open-minded, let each language shine\n        in its lane, and you'll find that React, PHP, and Go aren't just compatible —\n        they're a delight.\n      </p>\n\n      <p>\n        Now go build something cool with your whole squad. 🚀\n      </p>\n    </article>\n  </div>\n\n  <footer>\n    <div class=\"container\">\n      © 2026 language-tests · Written because tech doesn't have to fight.\n    </div>\n  </footer>\n</body>\n</html>\n"}}]},{"role":"tool","content":[{"type":"tool-result","toolCallId":"call_5aa5e7edcec244deaef54a68","toolName":"write_file","output":{"type":"text","value":"Written: /Users/jeffrey/www/language-tests/articles/react-php-go-friends.html"}}]},{"role":"assistant","content":"Created `articles/react-php-go-friends.html` — a complete styled blog post referencing the image (via `../images/react-php-go-friends.png`). It starts, does its job, and exits — usually in milliseconds.\n        That lifecycle is what makes it so reliable for web requests. But it's also a\n        <strong>limitation</strong>. Some tasks aren't finished in a single request. Some\n        jobs need to keep running — polling a queue, processing files, waiting for a WebSocket\n        connection — long after PHP has packed up and gone home.\n      </p>\n\n      <p>\n        That's where Go steps in. New content:

​```
      <h2>Wiring It Into a Page — Without Busy-Polling</h2>
      <p>
        A subtle trap: if you "wait" on <code>go_status()</code> — looping and re-checking
        every few seconds — you've just recreated the long-running process <em>inside PHP</em>.
        That defeats the whole point. PHP should make <strong>one</strong> check, at the moment
        you actually expect the result to be ready.
      </p>

      <p>
        Since you know your Go process takes about two minutes, schedule a single check to
        fire at the three-minute mark. Use a tiny scheduler table plus a cron job. PHP stores
        the job, sets a <code>due_at</code> timestamp, and moves on. Later, cron runs once and
        calls <code>go_status()</code> a single time.
      </p>

      <pre><code><span class="lang">PHP — submit.php (enqueue, don't wait)</span>&lt;?php

$upload  = $_POST['file_to_process'] ?? '';
$jobId   = go_submit($upload);                 // fires the job, returns fast

// Known runtime ≈ 2 min → check once, 3 minutes from now.
$dueAt   = date('Y-m-d H:i:s', time() + 180);

// Save so a scheduled cron can find it later.
$pdo->prepare('INSERT INTO jobs (job_id, due_at) VALUES (?, ?)')
    ->execute([$jobId, $dueAt]);

echo "Job queued! We'll email you when it's done (ID: {$jobId}).";

// ---- scheduler.php — run by cron every minute ----
$rows = $pdo->query('SELECT job_id FROM jobs WHERE due_at <= NOW()')->fetchAll();

foreach ($rows as $row) {
    $status = go_status($row['job_id']);       // one check, no busy loop
    if ($status !== null) {
        notify_user($row['job_id'], $status);  // email / websocket / etc.
        $pdo->prepare('DELETE FROM jobs WHERE job_id = ?')->execute([$row['job_id']]);
    }
}</code></pre>

      <blockquote>
        The user sees an instant response. The long task runs in Go and is checked <strong>exactly
        once</strong>, at a time you chose. PHP never blocks, never polls in a loop — it just shows
        up when the work is presumably done.
      </blockquote>
​```

The user is making a good technical point. Let me think about it carefully.

The user disagrees with my polling approach. Their argument: my `go_status` polling approach in PHP still involves blocking/waiting — a long-running request hanging around. Instead they'd prefer a queue-based approach with cron, checking at a set interval (e.g., the process takes 2 minutes, so PHP checks `go_status` in 3 minutes — a delayed check rather than tight polling).

They ask "No response? You don't agree?"

I should engage genuinely with the technical point. Let me think about whether they're right.

My original code had PHP poll `go_status` — and in the actual PHP usage I showed a page that returns immediately with "processing". The polling loop wasn't really shown in a blocking way. But conceptually, the "poll for the result" pattern does create repeated requests. The user is right that frequent polling is wasteful and can keep connections busy.

Their suggestion: use a queue approach + cron, check at a determined interval. E.g., process takes 2 minutes → cron checks `go_status` every 3 minutes. This is "delayed" or "batched" polling rather than continuous polling. This avoids tight-loop spinning and lets the web thread return immediately.

The user distinguishes this from my polling which they interpret as a potentially long/synchronous wait.

Let me evaluate:

- Continuous polling: HTTP request fires, if not done yet, it re-fires immediately in a loop within a request → can hang/block that PHP worker/connection. That's genuinely bad. My sample text actually said "Later, poll for the result" — which could be read as looping.
- Delayed polling via cron (cron-driven status check): better. PHP request returns instantly, cron job (separate process, not a user-facing request) checks `go_status` at set intervals and completes the job when ready.
- True async queue (Redis/RabbitMQ): even better decoupling; Go consumes queue directly, no PHP polling at all. PHP just enqueues.

So the user is right — scheduling the check (cron at a set interval) is better than tight polling within a user request. I should agree with them and revise the article to reflect this. This aligns well with my own "Operational Notes" nod toward queues.

I should:
1. Acknowledge they're right — I was writing it loose and the "poll" wording did invite a bad blocking-loop read.
2. Update the article to prefer the cron/delayed-check approach, and strengthen the queue as the best option.
3. Maybe add a cron section with examples (a check script + crontab line, e.g., every 3 minutes for a 2-minute job).

Let me craft the cron section:

A separate PHP CLI script `check_jobs.php` that cron runs. It calls go_status for known pending jobs and finalizes them when ready.

​```php
<?php
// check_jobs.php — run by cron, not by a user request

$pendingIds = getPendingJobsFromDatabase(); // db of queued job ids

foreach ($pendingIds as $jobId) {
    $status = go_status($jobId);
    if ($status !== null) {
        markJobCompleteInDatabase($jobId, $status);
    }
}
​```

crontab:
​```
*/3 * * * * php /var/www/scripts/check_jobs.php
​```

For a job that takes 2 minutes, running every 3 minutes means results checked at most 3 minutes after — the batch interval is >= process duration so you're not spinning.

This addresses the user's "max/min time" point — set the cron interval to a determined time (>= process runtime).

Checking <code>go_status</code> from\n        inside the user request is tempting but wrong. If you \"poll\" that way —\n        firing repeated <code>go_status</code> calls until the job is done — you've just\n        recreated the long-running request you were trying to avoid. The right move is to check\n        <em>once, later</em>, on a schedule that outlasts the job. A cron job is perfect for\n        this: it runs in its own process, not in a web request, so nothing in the front end\n        ever waits.\n      </p>\n\n      <pre><code><span class=\"lang\">PHP CLI — check_jobs.php (run by cron, not by a user)</span>&lt;?php\n\n// Find IDs we queued but haven't finished yet.\n$pending = get_pending_jobs();\n\nforeach ($pending as $job) {\n    $status = go_status($job['go_id']);\n\n    if ($status !== null) {\n        mark_job_complete($job['id'], $status);\n    }\n}</code></pre>\n\n      <p>And the cron line — for a job that runs two minutes, check every three:</p>\n\n      <pre><code><span class=\"lang\">crontab</span>*/3 * * * * php /var/www/scripts/check_jobs.php</pre></code>\n\n      <p>\n        Set the interval a little <em>longer</em> than the longest job. The batch pass finds\n        finished jobs and records their results; anything still running just waits for the\n        next pass. The web request never blocks, the PHP worker is free, and Go keeps\n        simmering in the background on its own time.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>Operational Notes for the Real World</h2>"}
```

Returns stdout, stderr, and exit code.\n\nSupported commands:\n  - ls: list files in a directory (flags like -la ok). Read-only.\n  - tree: print a directory tree. Read-only. Read-only.\n  - git: status/log/diff/show/branch (read), add/commit/checkout/reset (mutate — will prompt), push/pull/fetch (network — will prompt). You're exactly right. Tight-polling `go_status` from inside a web request just rebuilds the long-running request we were avoiding. I rewrote that section to replace blocking/polling with a scheduled (cron) check that runs after the job completes — no user request ever waits.

Let me also add a matching caveat to the operational notes so the messaging is consistent:


```

font-size: 1.12rem; }\n\n    p { margin: 16px 0; }\n\n    ul, ol { padding-left: 24px; }\n\n    li { margin: 8px 0; }\n\n    code {\n      background: #eef0f7;\n      border: 1px solid var(--border);\n      border-radius: 6px;\n      padding: 2px 6px;\n      font-family: \"SF Mono\", \"Menlo\", \"Consolas\", monospace;\n      font-size: 0.9em;\n    }\n\n    pre {\n      background: #1b1f2b;\n      color: #e6edf3;\n      padding: 18px;\n      border-radius: var(--radius);\n      overflow-x: auto;\n      font-size: 0.9rem;\n      line-height: 1.6;\n    }\n\n    pre code {\n      background: none;\n      border: none;\n      color: inherit;\n      padding: 0;\n    }\n\n    pre .lang {\n      display: block;\n      font-size: 0.75rem;\n      letter-spacing: 0.1em;\n      text-transform: uppercase;\n      color: #8b93a7;\n      margin-bottom: 10px;\n    }\n\n    blockquote {\n      margin: 24px 0;\n      padding: 14px 20px;\n      border-left: 4px solid var(--accent);\n      background: #e4f6fb;\n      border-radius: 0 var(--radius) var(--radius) 0;\n      color: var(--muted);\n    }\n\n    .note {\n      background: var(--surface);\n      border: 1px solid var(--border);\n      border-left: 4px solid var(--php);\n      border-radius: var(--radius);\n      padding: 14px 18px;\n      margin: 20px 0;\n      font-size: 0.93rem;\n      color: var(--muted);\n    }\n\n    table.timeline {\n      width: 100%;\n      border-collapse: collapse;\n      margin: 20px 0;\n      font-size: 0.92rem;\n    }\n\n    table.timeline th, table.timeline td {\n      text-align: left;\n      padding: 10px 12px;\n      border-bottom: 1px solid var(--border);\n    }\n\n    table.timeline th { color: var(--muted); It starts, does its job, and exits — usually in milliseconds.\n        That lifecycle is what makes it so reliable for web requests. But it's also a\n        <strong>limitation</strong>. Some tasks aren't finished in a single request. Some\n        jobs need to keep running — polling a queue, processing files, waiting for a WebSocket\n        connection — long after PHP has packed up and gone home.\n      </p>\n\n      <p>\n        That's where Go steps in. We already established that Go makes a great friend. Now\n        let's give it a <em>job</em>: run a long-lived process, and let PHP knock on its door\n        whenever something needs doing.\n      </p>\n\n      <h2>Why a Long-Running Process Can't Live in PHP</h2>\n      <p>\n        Every PHP request is effectively born and dies in the time it takes to render one\n        page. **Response:**

```
Also, the "Wiring It Into a Page" section now references functions like `save_pending_job` and the cron section uses `get_pending_jobs`/`mark_job_complete`. Those helper names are consistent enough to read as illustrative, so I'll leave the examples self-contained.

Updates made to `articles/go-microservice-php.html`:

1. **Replaced the blocking/polling pattern** in the "Wiring It Into a Page" flow — it now records the job as pending and returns immediately, with a clear note that completion happens later on a schedule.
2. **Added a new section "Completing Jobs on a Schedule — Not on a Request"** explaining why tight-polling `go_status` is wrong, with a cron-run PHP CLI script and a `*/3` crontab example (checked after the 2-minute job, per your 3-minute suggestion).
3. **Two new operational tips**: never block the request with polling, and de-duplicate/keep cron passes idempotent.

Thanks for the catch — you read it right, and this approach is substantially better for keeping PHP web requests fast.
```
